SUMMARY

Climate risk in agriculture represents the probability of a defined hydro-meteorological hazard
affecting the livelihood of farmers. Risk refers to a probability that can be estimated from prior
information, while uncertainty applies to situations in which probability cannot be estimated.
Both risks and uncertainties contribute to choice of appropriate management practices by the
decision-makers in agriculture. Farmers to some extent understand the risks and uncertainties
of climate at their location and optimize the management practices based on years of
experience. However, growing demand, changing climatic conditions, intensification and
spread of agriculture to marginal production environments warrants improved climate risk
management and decision support systems to enable appropriate choice of practices and
strategies to match the current and future climate risks.

Bangladesh with a relatively large share of population dependent on agriculture is more prone
to the adverse effects of climatic variability. Historical trends and a number of model based
projections point to a noticeable increase in surface temperature and erratic rainfall in
Bangladesh. This along with the possible changes in spatial and temporal patterns in rainfall
poses challenges to sustainable agricultural production. Keeping the need to make Bangladesh
agriculture more resilient to changing and increasingly variable climate. In order to develop
and target appropriate adaptation measures, it is important to identify regions that are more
affected by climatic variability. This identification process involves assessment of vulnerability
of different upazilas in Bangladesh. This proposed risk mapping is presented the analysis of
vulnerability of agriculture to climate variability and risks at the upazila level considering the
fact that most of the development planning and programme implementation is done at upazila
level in Bangladesh. The output of the analysis has been presented in the form of maps
representing various dimensions of vulnerability. This approach is useful in identifying the
upazilas that are vulnerable to climate so that necessary management practices and adoption of
technologies could be targeted. It is also useful in identifying sources of vulnerability that are
critical to developing appropriate adaptation measures in terms of technologies, investments
and polices. With the above background, this study has been conducted with the aim to identify
the natural and climate induced vulnerability of the upazilas level to determine the highest
vulnerable areas and provide the intervention benefits of the formers against various degrees of
risk and vulnerability. Presented below the major conclusions based on the study:

(i)
This study, shows that most of the cyclone hit around Chottagram-Feni coast and
these coastal areas was observed as at very high risk. On the other hand, only high
risk shows in Barguna-Patuakhali-Noakhali-Bhola coast, whereas Khulna coast
areas show low risk area, and other areas show very low to no risk. Upazilas affected
by the cyclone at different intensities in the above-areas.
(ii)
Haor region is found very high to high risk area for flash flood. On the other hand,
only high risk area is noticed over the upazilas under Madaripur, Shariatpur, Rajbari,
Faridpur, Manikganj, Jamalpur and Gaibandha zilas. The rest of the zilas are
comparatively less vulnerable.

`

(iii)
The study observes that very high vulnerable area for cold wave in the upazilas
under Rajshahi-Dinajpur regions. The upazilas under Chuadanga, Meherpur,
Kushtia, Natore, Bogura, Guaibandha, Rangpur, Kurigram, Nilphamri, Lalmonirhat
and Moulvibazar zilas are high vulnerable area. The rest locations (upazilas) is
found to be low to moderate vulnerable area for cold wave stress.
(iv)
High to very high heat wave vulnerable area is observed in the upazilas in the
western part; most of the upazilas are found to be low to moderate heat wave
vulnerable area in Bangladesh except the southern parts costal area; the latter region,
is classed as no heat waves vulnerable area.
(v)
High to very high salinity intrusion is observed over almost all upazilas under
Satkhira, Khulna, Barguna, Pirojpur, Jhalokati, Patuakhali, Bhola Noakhali and
Cox’s Bazar zilas and low to moderate over the upazilas under Narial, Gopalganj,
Madaripur, Shariatpur, Chanpur, Lakshimpur, Feni and Chittagong zilas.
(vi)
The produced vulnerable map with respect to storm surge inundation, the designates
very high category upazilas in the mid-southern and southeast part (Patuakhali,
Bhola, Noakhali, Chattogram zilas) and high category upazilas in the Satkhira,
Khulna, Bagerhat, Chandpur, Lakshimipur, Barishal, Bhola, Narsingdi, Cox’s
Bazar, Shariatpur, zilas; the rest coastal locations (upazilas) are classed as low to
moderate vulnerable.
(vii)
With respect to the amount of 1-day maximum rainfall, very high risk area is
categorized for some upazilas in the southeastern part of Chattogram and Bhola
zilas, high risk area is found to be in some upazilas of Mymensingh, Chattogram.
Noakhali, Sylhet and Dinajpur zilas, and low to moderate risk area in other
locations; this categorization has been based on observed rainfall (BMD data).
Using gridded Aphrodite rainfall data, high to very high risk areas have been
designated for some upazilas of Sylhet, Sunamganj and Cox’s Bazar zilas, whereas,
low to moderate risk areas is demarked over the upazilas of southern and
northeastern, Bangladesh and the rest of the upazilas (notably western part) classed
as low risk area. With CRU rainfall data, upazilas under Noakhali, Bhola,
Sunamganj and Sylhet zilas belonged to very high to high risk areas, whereas, study
locations of southeast to central part is designated to be low to moderate risk area.
The risk categorization using CRU rainfall was similar to that using the Aphrodite
gridded rainfall data-source.
(viii) Vulnerability Index (SPI) based on monsoon drought analysis using observed
gridded Aphrodite rainfall, indicates the prevalence of severe to extremely dry
conditions in most of the upazilas over southern and western part of Bangladesh;
some upazilas of northwest and some isolated upazilas of eastern part showed mild
wet conditions, whereas extreme wet conditions were seen in some upazilas of
Sylhet region. Furthermore, mild dry conditions prevailed in some pocket upazilas
of northern part of Bangladesh. The gridded Aphrodite rainfall based SPI was very
similar to observed rainfall.
Projections
(ix)
Vulnerability Index (SPI) based on monsoon seasonal drought frequency is likely
to decrease over northwest upazilas of Bangladesh under RCP4.5. Mild to moderate

`

drought conditions would prevail over northwest upazilas of Bangladesh. On the
other hand, moderate to extremely wet conditions are expected over southern
upazilas and mild wet conditions may prevail over narrow part of central upazilas
(along Meherpur-Rajshahi to Sylhet-Moulvibazar) in 2030. But in 2050 in southern
regional upazilas are projected to experience mild to moderate wet conditions and
central to northern (including Chapai Nawabganj, Rajshahi, Meherpur and
Chuadanga zilas) upazilas to experience severe to extreme wet conditions in 2050.
In monsoon season, mild wet conditions are projected to occur in most of the
upazilas in Bangladesh except in some upazilas of western part (Rajshahi regions),
it is likely to occur moderate to severe wet conditions in 2070. On the other hand,
mild to moderate dry conditions are expected over north-eastern and southern
upazilas at the same year. Under RCP4.5, severe to extreme wet conditions are
projected to increase in almost all upazilas (central to southern part) for monsoon
season in 2090, whereas, mild to moderate wet conditions are likely to occur over
northern upazilas as the same period.
(x)
Monthly rainfall is expected to decrease by more than -10% in most of the months
except in January, May, July, August, October and December, where July and
October is likely to increase by more than 10% and in rest of the months is projected
to remain the same in most of the upazilas in 2030. Seasonal rainfall is likely to
decrease by more than -10% during pre-monsoon and winter season; monsoonal and
post-monsoonal rainfall is expected to rise or stay normal for most of the upazilas
compared to the baseline period. Annual rainfall is expected to normal in most of
the upazilas under RCP4.5 in 2030.
(xi)
During 2050, monthly rainfall is projected to remain the same in most of the months
except in March, April, June, July, August and September, where March is expected
to decrease by more than -10% and in rest of the months is likely to increase by
more than 10% in most of the upazilas in 2050. Seasonal rainfall is likely to remain
the same during pre-monsoon, post-monsoon and winter season; the monsoonal
rainfall is expected to rise by more than 10% for most of the upazilas compared to
the baseline period. Annual rainfall is expected to increase by more than 10% in
most of the upazilas under RCP4.5 in 2050.
(xii)
Monthly rainfall is expected to decrease by more than -10% in most of the months
except in May, July, August and November, where it is likely to increase by more
than 10% in most of the upazilas in 2070. Seasonal rainfall is likely to decrease by
more than -10% during post-monsoon and winter season; pre-monsoonal and
monsoonal rainfall is expected to rise or stay normal for most of the upazilas
compared to the baseline period. Annual rainfall is expected to normal in most of
the upazilas under RCP4.5 in 2070.
(xiii) Monthly rainfall is likely to increase by more than 10% in most of the months except
in March, April, June, and December, where it is likely to decrease by more than -
10% in most of the upazilas in 2090. Seasonal rainfall is likely to increase by more
than 10% during monsoon, post-monsoon and winter seasons; the pre-monsoonal
rainfall is expected to decrease for most of the upazilas compared to the baseline

`

period. Annual rainfall is expected to increase in most of the upazilas under RCP4.5
in 2090.
(xiv) Monthly maximum temperature is projected to increase between 0.3 to 1.8oC under
RCP4.5. The highest increase in maximum temperature in March is projected by
about 1.8oC in the upazilas of northeast part (Sylhet region), Bangladesh. Seasonal
maximum temperature is expected to rise in the range of 0.9-1.8oC, 0.8-1.3oC, 0.9-
1.4oC and 1.0-1.7oC for pre-monsoon, monsoon, post-monsoon and winter season,
respectively. Annual maximum temperature is projected to rise, on an average by
1.3oC for the year 2030. Similarly, monthly minimum temperature is expected to
rise by 0.2 to 1.9oC. The highest minimum temperature in December and February
is expected to increase by 1.3oC over northwestern upazilas (Kurigram region).
Seasonal minimum temperature is projected to rise in the range of 0.7 to 1.6oC, 0.7
to 1.3oC, 0.4 to 0.9oC and 0.7 to 1.7oC for pre-monsoon, monsoon, post-monsoon
and winter season, respectively. Annual minimum temperature is likely to increase,
on an average by 1.1oC under RCP4.5 in 2030.
(xv)
The change of maximum temperature in various months at different upazilas is
projected to increase by about 0.5 to 2.0oC for the year 2050. The highest increase
in maximum temperature in March is projected by about 2.0oC over northwestern
upazilas. Seasonal maximum temperature is likely to increase in the range of 1.3-
1.7oC, 1.1-1.8oC, 1.5-2.2oC and 1.1-1.7oC for pre-monsoon, monsoon, postmonsoon winter season, respectively. Annual maximum temperature is projected to
increase, on an average by 1.6oC under RCP4.5. Similarly, minimum temperature
change in different months are expected to rise by 1.0 to 2.6oC over Bangladesh.
The highest in minimum temperature could increase by 2.6oC in December over
western upazilas (Rajshahi region). The rate of increase in seasonal minimum
temperature can occur in the range of 1.1-1.7oC, 1.2-1.7oC, 1.4-2.0oC and 1.2-2.0oC
for pre-monsoon, monsoon, post-monsoon winter season, respectively and annual

CHAPTER 1

INTRODUCTION

1.1
Introduction

Climate change is one of the current issues that severely impact all climate sensitive sectors
like agriculture. The manifestation of climate change such as rising temperatures, increasingly
erratic rainfall, and more frequent and severe floods and droughts have grave consequences on
the livelihood security of smallholder farming communities, making them more vulnerable.
Agriculture plays a great role in the livelihood of rural communities in many Asian countries.
Most such countries are, however, predicted to be among the globe’s most vulnerable to
climatic changes (Schlender and Lobell, 2010; Samson et al., 2011; Morand et al., 2012).
Scientists noted that the negative consequences of climate change are anticipated overall for
Asia where most of the small farmers subsist on rain-fed agriculture. In Bangladesh, agriculture
is the dominant sector contributing around 13% of the Gross Domestic Product (GDP) and 70%
of total employment and livelihoods. Crop production is dominated by small scale subsistence
farmers who practice more traditional farming. The most vulnerable households are those with
assets and livelihoods exposed and sensitive to climatic risks, and who have weak risk
management capacity (Heltberg et al., 2009).

Assessment and mapping of the vulnerability to climate change is the base for the development
of site specific adaptation options that reduce the risks associated with climate change. Several
researchers have noted that vulnerability mapping including exposure, sensitivity, and adaptive
capacity, has become a central tool for communicating with policy makers and local
stakeholders as well as visualising climate change impacts on the landscape to more effectively
support risk management and spatial planning (Eakin and Luers, 2006; Preston et al., 2011).
Vulnerability assessment describes a diverse set of methods used to systematically integrate
and examine interactions between humans and their physical and social surroundings. The level
of vulnerability of different areas to climate change is determined by both socioeconomic and
environmental factors. The socioeconomic factors include the level of technological
development, infrastructure and institutional linkage (Kelly and Adger 2000; McCarthy et al.,
2001); while the environmental attributes are climatic conditions, quality of soil, and
availability of water for irrigation (O’Brien et al., 2004). The variations of these socioeconomic
and environmental factors across different social groups are responsible for the differences in
their levels of vulnerability and coping capacities to climate change. Bangladesh is almost
homogeneous in elevation, climate, agricultural production, cultural practices and other socioeconomic factors. The degrees therefore of vulnerabilities of different localities and farming
systems are not varied accordingly.

Disaster vulnerability lies under the circumstances affecting the social and economic structures
of the society (Morrow 1999; de Chazal et al. 2008). Natural and climate-induced disasters are
considered as the coincidences between hazardous events (such as cyclones, floods, droughts,
and sea-level rise), elements at risk (such as environmental, financial, and political systems),

`

and conditions of vulnerability of any system (Maskrey 1989; Birkmann 2011). The
susceptibility to the risk of any disaster invariably becomes higher when one or more natural
hazards occur in a vulnerable region (Wisner & Luce 1993). The theoretical conceptualization
of vulnerability assessments to identify disaster risk zones have often been used as the basis for
pre-impact studies and mitigation planning strategies (Brooks et al. 2005; Cutter et al. 2008).
Various sectors, such as agriculture, forests, water resources and coastal regions have been
projected to have several catastrophic impacts due to natural and climate-induced disasters. The
severity of these impacts are further exacerbated by the current vulnerability of any region from
single stressors (hazards) to multiple stressors (global change) (Helmer & Hilhorst 2006, Van
Aalst 2006). The distribution of these incidences, however, varies with the geography
encompassed throughout the entire globe (Cutter et al. 2008). In addition to this, multidimensional assessments of such impacts also vary across at different levels and scales of
assessments (O’Brien et al. 2004). Therefore, understanding the cumulative impacts at the subdistrict and district level to natural and climate-induced disasters is essential to decipher the
complex processes that address human rights and humanitarian development. The prior
knowledge of vulnerability of the system and ways to increase its adaptive capacity would help
in reducing the adverse risks due to disasters. In resource-constrained countries, disaster
vulnerability assessments will help in identifying and enhancing the actions required for
adaptation and mitigation strategies for the most vulnerable communities.

The long-term developmental processes in Bangladesh would be greatly influenced by the
frequency and magnitude of the occurrence of natural and climate-induced disasters. With a
wide variety of physical and climatic features, the vast diversity in Bangladesh leads to different
disaster-prone scenarios in the country. One of the major reasons for such a conclusion is the
inadequate database and community or sector-specific adaptation and mitigation strategies
(Guha-Sapir D, et al. 2013). The identification and characterization of the vulnerable
communities and sectors are essential for targeting and planning any policy intervention.

The Principal Component Analysis (PCA) has been widely used to solve a broad range of multicriteria decision-making problems, with the comparison matrix calculating the weights for each
criterion considered. The flexibility of using PCA over multi-criteria methods brings intuitive
appeal to decide solutions for integrated processes, with the enhanced ability to check
inconsistencies of each criterion considered to study environmental impacts (Ramanathan
2001). The uniqueness of applying PCA in different studies helps in modelling situations of
uncertainty without losing subjectivity and objectivity of any evaluation measure. Such
procedure-oriented methodology can easily incorporate tangible and non-tangible factors in
making major policy decisions.

With the aim of examining disasters under the concept of vulnerability, this study is an attempt
to map the upazilas in Bangladesh that are vulnerable to natural and climate-induced disasters.
Using PCA as a multi-criteria decision-mapping method, vulnerability measured in terms of
exposure, sensitivity, and adaptive capacity was assessed for entire country at the upazila level.
The results of this study was to determine the degree of climate risk and the relative

`

vulnerability of the farming areas of Bangladesh to climate change by developing upazila level
vulnerability maps that identify the most vulnerable hotspots.

1.2
Vulnerability

The ordinary use of the word ‘vulnerability’ refers to the capacity to be wounded, i.e., the
degree to which a system is likely to experience harm due to exposure to a hazard. A sample of
definitions on vulnerability is summarized below focusing different issues:

Gabor and Griffith (1979) referred vulnerability as a threat to which a community is exposed,
taking in to account not only the properties of the chemical agents involved but, also the
ecological situation of the community and the general state of emergency preparedness, at any
point in time.

Timmerman (1981) came up with his new definition incorporating coping capability. He
defined vulnerability as the degree to which a system acts adversely to the occurrence of a
hazardous event. The degree and quality of the adverse reaction are conditioned by a system’s
resilience (a measure of the system’s capacity to absorb and recover from the event).

Cutter (1996) defined vulnerability as the likelihood that an individual or group will be exposed
to and adversely affected by a hazard. It is the interaction of the hazards of the place (risk and
mitigation) with the social profile of the communities.

George E Clark (1998) defined vulnerability as a function of two attributes: 1) exposure (the
risk of experiencing a hazardous event); and 2) coping ability, subdivided into resistance (the
ability to absorb impacts and continue functioning), and resilience (the ability to recover from
losses after an impact).

The IPCC definition of vulnerability (2001): Vulnerability is “the degree, to which a system is
susceptible to, or unable to cope with, adverse effects of climate change, including climate
variability and extremes”. There are three components of vulnerability. As per the IPCC
definition, vulnerability to climate change is a function of:

Exposure refers to the exposure of a system of interest to stimuli that act on that system. This
can be readily conceptualized as climate variability and/or the various changes in the climate
system that are often of concern to stakeholders: temperature increases, rainfall variability and
change (including extremes), or changes in the frequency or intensity of tropical cyclones.

Sensitivity refers to the responsiveness of a system to climate hazards. This is often represented
conceptually as a dose-response model – the more sensitive a system, the larger the rate or
magnitude of an adverse response to a given hazard. Sensitivity may vary considerably from
one system, sector or population to another. e.g., how much land could be inundated by sea
level rise, how much crop yields might change?

`

Adaptive capacity refers to the ability of a system to change in a way that makes it better
equipped to manage its exposure and/or sensitivity to climatic influences. Vulnerability is a
function of the degree of the system’s Exposure to climate hazards; it’s Sensitivity to such
hazards; and its Adaptive Capacity (IPCC, 2001). Sensitivity and Exposure may be taken
together to yield Potential Impact (Allison et al., 2009).

1.3
Vulnerability Assessment

Vulnerability assessment is significant for current and future planning exercises. It is a way of
establishing who is vulnerable, where they are and what the strategies to combat vulnerability
are. It helps decision-makers in government, donor agencies and nongovernmental organization
to work for vulnerabilities of people. Proper risk management, preparedness and critical
decision making are all essential if the most vulnerable people are to be given the assistance
they need. A vulnerability assessment is the process of identifying, quantifying, and prioritizing
(or ranking) the vulnerabilities in a system. Assessment of vulnerability to climate variability
and change broadly helps in understanding current vulnerability, identifying the factors that
render some districts more vulnerable than others, inform and facilitate the decision-making
process and selection of adaptation strategies and practices.

1.4
Vulnerability and Climate Change

Climate change impacts result from the interaction between climate and non-climate drivers
and have significant regional variations (Nicholls et. al., 2008). So, land and coastal
vulnerability analysis should adopt an integrated approach which considers natural climate
variability and climate-induced disasters. There are many uncertainties regarding the future
climate projections and impacts of climate change, yet, several studies have been conducted to
determine the climate change vulnerability in broader scale. But, none of them addressed the
upazila level vulnerability in both natural system and socio-economic context. Most of the
reports and studies on climate change vulnerability have addressed the different natural hazards
and their impacts on the whole country or on the coastal districts in qualitative and quantitative
approach (Ali, 1999; Karim and Mimura, 2008). Some of them focused on the longer-term
challenge of building future resilience and sustainability in the communities by addressing the
causes of vulnerability (Pomeroy et al., 2006). This study has been performed to address the
vulnerability of the upazilas in Bangladesh against the common natural hazards and climateinduced disasters to finally select the most vulnerable upazilas/zones for performing the
development activities.

1.5
Objectives of the study

The objectives of the study are as follows:

(i)
The objective of Vulnerability Assessment & Climate Risk Mapping of 487 upazilas
of Bangladesh is to show which upazila is more vulnerable or less vulnerable for

`

agricultural production, how these are already affected or likely to be affected in
future in different aspects on agricultural activities in Bangladesh.
(ii)
To reduce the loss of agricultural product and enhance production in relation to
climate information. This assessment and risk mapping is very important for these
upazilas and for the farmers and farming community to take necessary steps to
develop agricultural products and reduce loss of agricultural products from extreme
weather/climate events in future in Bangladesh.

1.6
Scope of works

 An in-depth analysis will be carried out to identify the vulnerability of the 487 upazilas
in Bangladesh against the natural system and climate-induced disasters.
 To make vulnerability index map for 487 upazilas based on the indicators related to
sensitivity, adaptive capacity and exposure based on the recent census data.
 To make vulnerability index map for 487 upazilas based on the seasonal drought event
using observed gridded data (1981-2015) (APHRODITE).
 To make vulnerability index map for 487 upazilas based on the seasonal drought event
to the projected changes in climate for 2030, 2050, 2070 and 2090 respectively,
relative to the baseline 1971-2000 using high resolution GCM scenario.
 Climate change vulnerability map will be made for 487 upazilas based on monthly,
seasonal projected change in rainfall and temperature (max. & min.) using historical
model data (1971-2000) from GCM as base period and for 2030, 2050, 2070 and 2090
respectively as future projection.
 To make vulnerability map for 487 upazilas based on measured in terms of millimeters
of rain per rainy day, it represents in terms of change for 2030, 2050, 2070 and 2090
respectively, relative to baseline 1971-2000 using GCM scenario.
 To make vulnerability map for 64 districts by using NDVI, TCI, VCI through
NOAA/Landsat satellite data/information.
 As rainfed agriculture in Bangladesh depends on the effective soil moisture storage,
vulnerability map to be developed based on P/PE ratio (P= Precipitation, PE=Potential
Evapotranspiration) measurement under observed and futuristic projections.

CHAPTER 2

METHODOLOGY AND DATA COLLECTION

2.1
Methodology

The methodology describes how the concepts of exposure, sensitivity, adaptive capacity, and
vulnerability were translated into numerical indices; what variables were used; how variables
were aggregated into sub-indices and sub-indices into a composite vulnerability index. This
study has been carried out through determination of vulnerability index of 487 upazilas in
Bangladesh in terms of both natural system and climate-induced disasters. This vulnerability
index has been calculated for both the present situation that is the base scenario and also for the
future scenario under changed climate. Then, for both natural system and climate-induced
disasters indicators, Vulnerability Index (VI) has been obtained from certain formula for both
present and future scenarios. For present situation, climate-induced disasters analysis and
natural system vulnerability for 487 upazilas are determined by 11 (eleven) climate-induced
disasters indicators. After identification of the indicators, period of assessment, data collection,
cleaning and quality checking were done. Steps were followed to assess the vulnerability of the
upazilas are:

 Classification of indicator data into 3 categories - adaptive capacity, sensitivity and
exposure. Normalization of all the indicator data
 Determination of unbiased weights using Principal Component Analysis (PCA) to
calculate the weights of the indicators. Components with Eigen Value greater than 1
have been used to calculate the weights.
 Calculation of Vulnerability Index (VI) has been done.
 Ranking of upazillas based on the calculated index values. For example, rank 1: least
vulnerable (Highest index values), Rank 487: most vulnerable (Lowest index values).
 Classification of data or indicators into vulnerability range from 1 to 5 as 1 (No/Very
Low), as 2 (Low), 3 (Moderate), 4 (High) and 5 (Very High) respectively
 At last, grouping of the vulnerability index into three categories of cluster - (1) Low,
(2) Moderate and (3) High vulnerability.

All the steps of assessing vulnerability index for both natural system indicators and climateinduced disasters indicators, and also for the composite system, are shown in Fig. 2.1 as Flow
Chart.

The main natural system indicators were chosen from literature review and expert opinion and
also from the regular natural hazards occurring in the upazila areas. Exposure Indicators are:
(1) Storm surge inundation (m) (2) Inundation due to riverine flood (3) 1-day maximum rainfall
(mm) (4) Salinity intrusion due to SLR (%) (5) Drought (6) Cold wave (7) Heat wave (8) Hail
storm (9) Flash Flood (10) River flood.

`

Fig. 2.1: Steps of determining Vulnerability Index

2.2
Computation of Vulnerability Index

The approach of the study requires explicit selection of the indicator variables depicting the
best available proxies of disastrous events. The indicators selected for each of these elements
are essential variables for any kind of comprehensive vulnerability assessment. The composite
vulnerability index approach was used to map the upazilas/districts in Bangladesh vulnerable
to natural and climate-induced disasters. Vulnerability indices were calculated using data for
each upazila in Bangladesh. By aggregating the relative indices of exposure, sensitivity, and
adaptive capacity, the cumulative composite vulnerability index has been calculated to
categorize the highest and lowest vulnerable regions in Bangladesh at the upazila level.

Using the indicators described in above, the disaster vulnerability in Bangladesh was assessed.
Apart from the indicators selected for exposure, the spatial database was created for sensitivity
and adaptive capacity for each upazila/district. Since each indicator was measured on different
scales, dimension indices for each of the indicators for a upazila were calculated by normalizing
the data using the following equation:

Index =
X௔−X௠௜௡
X௠௔௫−X௠௜௡

`

where Index is the normalized value of an indicator, X௔is the actual value of the same indicator,
and X௠௜௡and X௠௔௫ are the minimum and maximum values, respectively, of the same indicator.

Three indices for sensitivity, exposure and adaptive capacity were constructed by obtaining a
weighted mean of the indicators identified. These three indices were averaged (with differential
weights) to obtain the vulnerability index, higher values of which indicate higher vulnerability
and lower values lower vulnerability. It is to be noted that this index is not an absolute measure
of damage or risk due to climate variability and it is only a relative measure of risk between the
upazilas. It is however helpful in targeting and prioritization of initiatives for adaptation.

2.3
Principal Component Analysis (PCA)

Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is a data exploration tool that converts a number of
potentially correlated variables (with some shared attributes, such as points in space or time)
into a set of uncorrelated variables that capture the variability in the underlying data. As such,
PCA can be used to highlight patterns within multivariable data. PCA is a non-parametric
analysis and is independent of any hypothesis about data probability distribution (Abdi &
Williams, 2010). PCA uses orthogonal linear transformation to identify a vector in N-
dimensional space that accounts for as much of the total variability in a set of N variables as
possible the first principal component (PC) where the total variability within the data is the sum
of the variances of the observed variables, when each variable has been transformed so that it
has a mean of zero and a variance of one (Hatcher, 1997). A second vector (second PC),
orthogonal to the first, is then sought that accounts for as much of the remaining variability as
possible in the original variables. Each succeeding PC is linearly uncorrelated to the others and
accounts for as much of the remaining variability as possible (Jolliffe, 2002). PCA can therefore
be used as descriptive, statistical approach to data transformation as a means of overcoming
variable incommensurability. The ranking of the PCs in order of their significance (based on
how much of the variability in the data they capture) is denoted by the Eigen values associated
with the vector for each PC. In the case of a spatially explicit analysis, each data point for each
variable is related to a specific point in space and the PCs derived from a PCA can be assigned
scores (synthetic variable values) for each of these points in space.

A PCA approach provides several potential advantages in the aggregation of spatially explicit,
potentially incommensurable variables. When the original variables are correlated then the
higher order PCs will capture more of the total variability in the data than any individual
original variable. Excluding the lower order PCs reduces the dimensionality (number of
variables) of the data while minimizing the loss of information (Smith, 2002). As such PCA
provides an approach to move from a large suite of individual indicators to a small number of
composite, units less indices (PCs), while reducing the trade-off between richness of
information and is communicability. Here it should be noted that the choice of the number of
dimensions (PCs) to be retained is subjective and is generally based on the interpretability of
the retained components (Srivastava, 2002), the proportion of the variation in the original
variables explained by the retained components and the shape of the scree and loading plots
(Griffith, et, al., 2000). PCA provides flexibility in the data reduction process through the

`

subjective choice of the number of PCs, and therefore indices, that are retained. As many
indices as usefully express important patterns in the multivariate data can be retained. In
contrast, a normalization approach to data aggregation collapses all indicators into a single
index. Finally, and perhaps most importantly, each PC can be related to the original variables
that the PC is most influenced by through the reported principal component loading factors.
The PCA loading factors associated with each retained PC allow the original variables to be
readily associated with the resulting ‘indices’, thus retaining important information regarding
the underlying drivers of the retained indices. All PCAs were undertaken using the R statistical
program. Pairwise correlation tests were applied in an attempt to reduce the initial set of metrics
to a smaller subset of non-highly correlated metrics (Lausch & Herzog, 2002; Schindler, et, al.,
2008). The Kaisere-Mayere-Olkin (KMO) sampling adequacy test values were >0.5 and
Bartlett’s sphericity tests returned p <0.05 for all PCA analyses, suggesting that the variables
were suitable for PCA analysis (Hair, et, al., 2006). A correlation matrix was used for the PCA
analyses as this standardizes the data and avoids potential bias resulting from the inclusion of
data with different scales and data ranges.

2.4
Calculation of unbiased weights

In the development of aggregated index, problems arise when the weights of each indicator
have to be selected. The indicators are assigned different weights determined by Principal
Component Analysis (PCA) to avoid the uncertainty of equal weighting given the diversity of
indicators used.

2.5
Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI) calculation

The SPI is an index developed by McKee et al. (1993) based on the probability of rainfall for
the time scale of interest and is relatively simple to compute. The time scale reflects the impact
of drought on the availability of different water resources. Soil moisture conditions respond to
rainfall anomalies on a relatively short scale. Groundwater, stream flow, and reservoir storage
reflect the longer-term rainfall anomalies. For the calculation of SPI for any location, long time
series of rainfall for the desired period is used. This long time series of rainfall is fitted to a
probability distribution, which is then transformed into a standardized normal distribution so
that the mean SPI for the location and desired period is zero. Positive SPI values indicate greater
than median rainfall and negative values indicate less than median rainfall. The classification
of the drought intensities based on the SPI value is as follows; moderately dry/moderate drought
for SPI from -1.0 to -1.49, severely dry/severe drought for SPI from -1.5 to -1.99 and extremely
dry/extreme drought for SPI of -2.0 and less.

In this study, for the seasonal rainfall series was fitted to the gamma distribution. The gamma
probability distribution function (pdf) is given as

f (x)=
ଵ
௕ೌ௰(௔) x௔ିଵeି
௫௕
⁄

`

for x > 0, where a > 0 &b > 0 are the shape and scale parameters, respectively, x > 0 is the
rainfall and Γ(a)is the gamma function.

The aim of fitting the distribution to the data is to estimate the parameters a & b. Integrating
pdf with respect to x and inserting the estimated values of a & b, the gamma cumulative
distribution function (CDF) is computed at each value of x. The CDF is then transformed into
the standard normal distribution to yield SPI.

2.6
NOAA satellite data procedure
2.6.1 Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI)

Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI) is widely used in drought studies. It is
generally used as an index of vegetation health and density.

NDVI =
ఒ௡௜௥ିఒ௥௘ௗ
ఒ௡௜௥ାఒ௥௘ௗ

Where λnir and λred are the reflectance in the near infrared and red bands, respectively. It
reflects vegetation intensity, Leaf Area Index (LAI) and biomass. It varies in a range between
-1 and +1. NDVI itself does not reflect drought or non-drought conditions. But the severity of
a drought may be defined as NDVI deviation from its long-term NDVI means (DEVNDVI). This
deviation is calculated as the difference between the NDVI for the current time step (e.g.
January 2001) and a long-term mean NDVI for that month (e.g. a 10-year long mean NDVI of
all Januaries from 2000 to 2009 for each pixel.

DEVNDVI = NDVIi −NDVImean ....... (1)

Where, NDVIi is the NDVI value for month i and NDVImean is the long-term NDVI for the
same month. When DEVNDVI is negative, it indicates the below-normal vegetation
condition/health and, therefore, suggests a prevailing drought situation. The greater the
negative departure from long-term means NDVI, the greater the magnitude of a drought. In
addition, the departure from the long-term mean NDVI also reflects the conditions of health of
vegetation in normal and wet months/years.

2.6.2 Vegetation Condition Index (VCI)

VCI shows how close the NDVI of the current month is to the minimum NDVI calculated
from the long-term record.

VCI = (
ே஽௏ூ௜ିே஽௏ூ௠௜
ே஽௏ூ௠௔௫ିே஽௏ூ௠௜)*100 ....... (2)

Where, NDVImax and NDVImin are calculated from the long-term record (e.g. 10 years) for that
month and j is the index of the current month of week. The 50% VCI indicate fair vegetation
conditions and the VCI values between 50 and 100% indicate optimal or above normal
conditions. Kogan (1995) illustrated that the VCI threshold of 35% may be used to identify

`

extreme drought conditions. The VCI value close to zero percent reflects an extremely dry
month, when the NDVI value is close to its long-term minimum. Low VCI values over several
consecutive time intervals point to drought development.

2.6.3 Temperature Condition Index (TCI)

TCI is based on the thermal band of AVHRR converted to brightness temperature (BT). TCI is
used to determine temperature-related vegetation stress and also stress caused by excessive
wetness. The TCI algorithm is similar to the VCI algorithm, and is given as

TCI = ቀ
஻்௠௔௫ି஻்
஻்௠௔௫ି஻்௠௜௡ቁ∗100 ......... (3)

Where BT, BTmax and BTmin are the smoothed monthly brightness temperature, multi-year
maximum and multi-year minimum, respectively, for each grid cell. The conditions are
estimated relative to the maximum and minimum temperature envelopes. The above formula
reflects different response of vegetation, to temperature. High temperatures in the middle of the
growing season indicate unfavorable conditions for drought, whilst low temperatures indicate
mostly favorable conditions.

Procedures
NOAA satellite NDVI and Brightness Temperature (BT) data of Bangladesh for 2006-2018
were downloaded from STAR global vegetation health products. The dataset contains weekly
NDVI and BT data, 52 images per year. The whole dataset contains 1352 images (676 NDVI,
676 BT).

Data for all upazilas were extracted from these images using upazila shapefile of Bangladesh.
For each upazila, minimum, maximum and mean NDVI and BT data were extracted. The
dataset was aggregated to estimate monthly, seasonal and annual values. Mean NDVI was
calculated using 12 years (2007-2018) mean NDVI data from each upazila. Then using eq. (2),
NDVI deviation was calculated for every year from 2007 to 2018. Monthly, seasonal and
annual maps were prepared to show the drought risk upazilas.

Using maximum and minimum NDVI data for upazilas, VCI values were estimated for each
upazila for every year from 2007 to 2018, using eq. (3). These VCI values were then classified
based on the condition of table 1. Monthly, seasonal and annual maps were prepared to show
the drought risk upazilas using these classified VCI values.

Using maximum and minimum BT data for upazilas, TCI values were estimated for each
upazila for every year from 2007 to 2018, using eq. (4). These TCI values were then classified
based on the condition of table 1. Monthly, seasonal and annual maps were prepared to show
the drought risk upazilas using these classified TCI values.

`

Table 2.1. Drought severity classes for TCI and VCI

Severity Class
Values
Extreme Drought
<10

<20

2.7
Data Collection

2.7.1 APHRODITE data

APHRODITE’s (Asian Precipitation-Highly-Resolved Observational Data Integration
Towards Evaluation) daily gridded (0.25 by 0.25) precipitation data has been collected from
their web site. After collection this data then extracted rainfall data as per latitude and longitude
of upazila level.

2.7.2 Primary and secondary data for risk map

Primary and secondary data for risk map has been collected from different sources like, BMD,
BBS, CEGIS, IWM, BWDB and BCAS and others sources.

2.7.3 Model data

The National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA) Earth Exchange (NEX) High
Resolution (25X25 km) Global Daily Downscaled Projections (NEX-GDDP) of six (6) models
(namely: CanESM2, GFDL-ESM2M, IPSL-CM5A-MR, MIROC-ESM., MPI-ESM-MR and
NorESM1) datasets have been collected from their web site and model data was extracted as
per upazilas latitude and longitude from the models in Bangladesh. Extracted upazila level
model data were compared with the observational data of BMD for rainfall and temperature. It
has been seen that NorESM1 model gives better result than the other models for rainfall and
temperature over Bangladesh.

The National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA) Earth Exchange Global Daily
Downscaled Projections (NEX-GDDP) dataset is comprised of downscaled climate scenarios
for the globe that are derived from the General Circulation Model (GCM) runs conducted under
the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5 (CMIP5) and across two RCPs (RCP 4.5
and RCP 8.5), of the four greenhouse gas emissions scenarios known as Representative
Concentration Pathways (RCPs). The CMIP5 GCM runs have been developed in support of the
Fifth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC AR5). The
purpose of these datasets is to provide a set of global daily, high resolution, bias-corrected
climate change projections that can be used to evaluate climate change impacts on processes

`

that are sensitive to finer-scale climate gradients and the effects of local topography on climate
conditions.

2.7.4 Satellite data

NOAA satellite (Landsat) NDVI, TCI and VCI data were used to downscale vulnerability
analysis to upazilas/districts level so that it can help in implementing mitigation and adaptation
strategies at the administrative-level. Thus, well thought-out strategic planning and coordinated
implementation is made to tackle impacts of climatic variability on Bangladesh agriculture.

2.7.5 Gridded Potential Evapotranspiration (PET) model data

Gridded rainfall and Potential Evapotranspiration (PET) data is collected from CORDEX web
site (NorSEM1 model data). This is high resolution (50km x50 km) climate data. After
collection this data then extracted rainfall and Evapotranspiration data as per latitude and
longitude of upazila level in Bangladesh.

`

CHAPTER 3

NATURAL AND CLIMTE-INDUCED VULNERABILITY INDICATOR MAPS

3.1
Vulnerability Maps

From the calculation of vulnerability index using Principal Component Analysis (PCs) several
risk maps have been generated showing vulnerability ranges. For 9 (nine) natural vulnerabilities
like cyclone, flash flood inundation, cold wave, heat wave, salinity intrusion, storm surge
inundation, riverine flood inundation, hail storm, 1-day maximum rainfall (mm). Risk maps
were prepared for all upazilas considering the present scenario and are presented with legends
in Fig. 3.1 to Fig. 3.6, respectively. Vulnerabilities are grouped into five categories of ranking
from 1 to 5, namely as No/Very Low Risk, Low Risk, Moderate Risk, High Risk and Very
High Risk, respectively as presented in Table 3.1.

3.1.1 Cyclone vulnerability map

The coastal regions of Bangladesh are subject to damaging cyclones almost every year. They
generally occur in early summer (April-May) or late rainy season (October-November).
Cyclones originate from low atmospheric pressures over the Bay of Bengal. Fig. 3.1 (upper)
shows that the Chittagong-Feni coast (namely upazilas are Rangunia, Sitakunda, Mirshwarai,
Patiya, Sandwip, Banshkhali, Boalkhali, Anowara, Chandanaish, Satkania, Lohagara,
Hathazari, Fatikchari, Raozan and Hatiya) is the very high vulnerable area for the landfall of
cyclones and high vulnerable area is found to Barguna-Patuakhali- Noakhali-Bhola coast, it is
located southern part of Bangladesh. The Barishal and Lakshmipur is the moderate vulnerable
coast. The Satkhira-Khulna/Sundarban and Cox’s Bazar coasts are relatively less vulnerable in
comparison to the above mentioned coasts.

3.1.2 Flash flood vulnerability map

Haor region is found very high to high risk area (namely upazilas: Sunamganj Sadar,

for flash flood and only high risk area is noticed in the upazilas of Madaripur, Shariatpur,

comparatively less vulnerable to the above mentioned zilas as shown in Fig 3.1 (lower).

vulnerable due to storm surge in Bangladesh.

`

3.1.7 Riverine flood vulnerability map

Riverine floods occur when the amount of runoff originating in a watershed (the area that
collects and directs the surface runoff into the rivers, streams and lakes that drain it) exceeds
the carrying capacity of natural and constructed drainage system. Flooding can occur due to
river overflow or surface runoff. When banks are overtopped, water spreads over the floodplain
and generally causes problems for inhabitants, crops and vegetation. As per vulnerability map
we have seen Brahmaputra river comes from north-western side in Bangladesh. This river area
is inundated almost every year. In Bangladesh side, Brahmaputra river name has become Jumna
river. Ganges side upazilas are: Belkuchi, Chauhali, Kamarkhand, Kazipur, Raigonj,
Shahjadpur, Sirajganj Sadar, Tarash, Ullapara, Sujananar, Ishurdi, Bhungura, Pabna Sadar,
Bera, Atghoria, Chatmohar, Sathia, Faridpur, Paba, Durgapur, Mohonpur, Charghat, Puthia,
Bagha, Godagari, Tanore, Bagmara, Natore Sadar, Singra, Baraigram, Bagatipara, Lalpur,
Gurudashpur, Naldanga, Chapainawabganj Sadar, Gomostapur, Nachole, Bholahat, Shibganj
and Meghna river side upazilas are: Lalmonirhat Sadar, Kaliganj, Hatibanda, Patgram,
Aditmari, Kurigram Sadar, Nageshari, Bhurungamari, Phulbari, Rajarhat, Ulipur, Chilmari,
Rowmari, Charrajibpur, Sadullapur, Gaibandha Sadar, Palashbari, Saghata, Gobindagonj,
Sundarganj, Phulchari, Baksiganj, Dewanganj, Islampur, Jamalpur Sadar, Maderganj,
Melandah, Sarishabari. These upazilas are found inundation area due to river flood as shown
in Fig. 3.4 (upper).

3.1.8 Hail storm vulnerability map

Very high risk area is observed over the upazilas of Sylhet regions due to hail among the others
area, which is normalized index 1.0. High risk area is found to be in most of the upazilas of
Rangpur, Bogura, Rajshahi divisions, Tangail, Hatiya, Chittagong, Rangmati as well as
Coxsbazar areas, which normalized index is 0.7. Moderate risk area is noticed in the upazilas
of Dhaka, Madaripur, Khulna, satkhira, Noakhali, Cumilla and Chandpur areas which is
normalized index is 0.4 as shown in Fig. 3.4 (lower) and in rest of the areas have shown almost
no risk.

Observer rainfall (BMD)

Very high risk area for 1-day maximum rainfall is observed in some upazilas of Chittagong
Bhola regions in southeastern parts (namely: Noakhali Sadar, Companiganj, Begumganj,
Subornochar, Kabirhat, Senbug, Chatkhil, Sonaimori, Sandip and Bhola Sadar) and high risk
area is found to be in the upazilas of Mymensingh, Chittagong, M. court, Srimongal and
Dinajpur regions and others area upazilas showed as low to moderate risk areas as shown in
Fig. 3.5 (upper).

4.1
Vulnerability index map based on seasonal drought analysis using observed

4.1.1 Vulnerability index map on seasonal drought for pre-monsoon season

Drought rainfall year-2014:

It has been selected three rainfall years of gridded Aphrodite data such as drought, normal and
excess rainfall year to see the vulnerability index based on seasonal drought using standardized
precipitation Index (SPI) from 1981-2015. Very recent drought rainfall year-2014 has been
taken for pre-monsoon season, it is observed that in a majority upazilas prevailed moderate to
severe dry conditions over Bangladesh except in some upazilas in southwestern and western
parts as well as in some isolated upazilas, where it showed mild dry conditions for the year
2014. Extremely dry conditions prevailed in some pocket upazilas in southeastern and northern
part in Bangladesh as presented in Fig. 4.1.

Normal rainfall year-2011

To see the vulnerability index based on seasonal drought using SPI. In this context, normal
rainfall year-2011 has been taken for pre-monsoon season, it is noticed that most of the upazilas
of Bangladesh prevail mild dry conditions except in some upazilas in southern and northeastern
part, where it is observed moderate to severe dry conditions for the year 2011 and mild wet
conditions prevailed over southeastern and some upazilas in northern part and some isolated
upazilas of Bangladesh for normal rainfall year as shown in Fig. 4.1.

Excess rainfall year-2013

To find out vulnerability index based on seasonal drought using SPI, in this respect, excess
rainfall year-2013 has been taken for pre-monsoon season, it is observed that over the upazilas
of northwestern parts prevail mild to moderate dry conditions and central to southern part
upazilas had seen mild to moderate wet conditions and severe wet conditions prevailed over
southern parts (Barishal and Cox’s Bazar region) upazilas of Bangladesh for excess rainfall
year in 2013 as presented in Fig. 4.2.

4.1.2 Vulnerability index map on seasonal drought for monsoon season

Drought rainfall year-2010

It has been selected three rainfall years of gridded Aphrodite data such as drought, normal and
excess rainfall year to see the vulnerability index based on seasonal drought using standardized

`

precipitation Index (SPI) from 1981-2015. Drought rainfall year-2010 has been chosen for

extremely dry conditions over southern and western part of Bangladesh except in some upazilas
in northwestern and some isolated upazilas in eastern part, where it is noticed mild wet
conditions for the year 2010. Extreme wet conditions had showed in some upazilas of Sylhet
regions. Mild dry conditions prevailed in some pocket upazilas of northern part of Bangladesh
as presented in Fig. 4.2.

Normal rainfall year-2011

To see the vulnerability index based on seasonal drought using SPI, in this context, normal
rainfall year-2011 has been selected for monsoon season from the 30-year gridded Aphrodite
rainfall, it is noticed that most of the upazilas prevailed mild wet conditions except in some
upazilas in southern and northern part, where it is found to be mild dry conditions for the year
2011 and moderate to severe wet conditions prevailed in some pockets upazilas in southwestern
and northeastern part of Bangladesh for normal rainfall year as shown in Fig. 3.3.

Excess rainfall year-2015

To find out vulnerability index based on seasonal drought using SPI. In this respect, excess
rainfall year-2015 has been selected for monsoon season, it is observed that over southern parts
upazilas prevailed moderate to severe wet conditions and extreme southern part in some
upazilas showed extremely wet conditions and central to northern part upazilas prevailed mild
wet conditions. Mild dry conditions also prevailed in some upazilas over northwest parts of
Bangladesh for excess rainfall year in 2015 as presented in Fig. 4.3.

CHAPTER 5

PROJECTED CHANGE IN VULNERABILITY INDEX MAP BASED ON
SEASONAL DROUGHT USING SPI UNDER RCP4.5 SCENARIO

5.1
Projected change in vulnerability index map based on seasonal drought using SPI
for the year 2030.
5.1.1 Seasonal drought for Pre-monsoon season for the year 2030

Pre-monsoon season is projected change in drought frequency under RCP4.5 all upazilas in
Bangladesh at different index in 2030 using NorESM model rainfall. Mild drought conditions
would prevail in most of the upazilas in Bangladesh except in southwestern and northeastern
part upazilas, where it may prevail moderate dry conditions in 2030 as shown in Fig. 5.1.

5.1.2 Seasonal drought for Monsoon season for the year 2030

Drought frequency in monsoon season is likely to increase under RCP4.5 over the upazilas of
northern part of Bangladesh in 2030 using NorESM model rainfall. Mild to moderate drought
conditions would prevail over the upazilas of northwestern part of Bangladesh. On the other
hand, moderate to extremely wet conditions are expected over the upazilas of southern part and
mild wet conditions may prevail over narrow part of central upazilas (along Meherpur-Rajshahi
to Sylhet-Moulvibazar) in 2030 as shown in Fig. 5.1.

5.1.3 Seasonal drought for Post-monsoon season for the year 2030

In post-monsoon season, change in drought frequency based on SPI is projected to decrease
over the western part upazilas in Bangladesh while the occurrence of drought based on SPI is
also projected to increase over the upazilas of northwestern and southeastern part under RCP4.5
using NorESM model rainfall. Mild wet conditions are expected in almost all upazilas except
in some upazilas of northwestern, southwestern and southeastern parts for the year 2030. Mild
dry conditions are likely to occur in a few upazilas of northwestern and southeastern part and
moderately wet conditions are expected to prevail over the upazilas of southwestern part in the
same year in Bangladesh as presented in Fig. 5.2.

5.1.4 Seasonal drought for Winter season for the year 2030

Winter season in 2030, change in drought frequency based on SPI is projected to increase in a
majority upazilas in Bangladesh under RCP4.5 using NorESM model rainfall. Mild dry
conditions are expected to occur in most of the upazilas in Bangladesh except in a few upazilas
of northwestern part and in some pockets upazilas, where it is likely to prevail moderate dry
conditions. Mild wet conditions are also expected to occur in the upazilas of southwestern part
of Bangladesh as presented in Fig. 5.2.

`

5.2
Projected change in vulnerability index map based on seasonal drought using SPI
for the year 2050
5.2.1 Seasonal drought for Pre-monsoon season for the year 2050

In pre-monsoon season, change in drought frequency based on SPI is projected to decrease over
the upazilas of southwestern part of Bangladesh while the occurrence of drought based on SPI
is also projected to increase in the upazilas of eastern part under RCP4.5 using NorESM model
rainfall. Mild wet conditions are likely to prevail over the upazilas of southwestern part
(Jashore-Khulna regions) and mild dry conditions are expected to occur in the upazilas of
northwestern, northeastern and southeastern part of Bangladesh for 2050 as presented in Fig.
5.3.

5.2.2 Seasonal drought for Monsoon season for the year 2050

Drought frequency in monsoon season is likely to decrease at different index under RCP4.5 in
2050 using NorESM model rainfall. Mild to moderate wet conditions is projected to prevail
over the upazilas of southern part of Bangladesh. Severe to extreme wet conditions are also
projected to occur in the upazilas of central to northern part (Chapai Nawabganj, Rajshahi,
Meherpur and Chuadanga zilas) in the same year as shown in Fig. 5.3.

5.2.3 Seasonal drought for Post-monsoon season for the year 2050

Post-monsoon season in 2050, change in drought frequency based on SPI is projected to decline
in a majority upazilas in Bangladesh under RCP4.5 using NorESM model rainfall. Mild wet
conditions are projected to occur in almost all upazilas in Bangladesh except in some upazilas
of northwestern and southeastern part, where it is likely to prevail dry conditions for the year
2050 as presented in Fig. 5.4. Moderate to severe wet conditions are expected to occur in some
pockets upazilas of Bogura, Natore, Sirajganj and Tangail regions in the same year.

5.2.4 Seasonal drought for Winter season for the year 2050

In winter season, Mild dry conditions are expected to occur in most of the upazilas in
Bangladesh except in some upazilas of southern and northwestern parts, where it is projected
to prevail mild wet conditions for the year 2050 as presented in Fig. 5.4.

Projected change in vulnerability index map based on seasonal drought using SPI
for the year 2070
5.3.1 Seasonal drought for Pre-monsoon season for the year 2070

In pre-monsoon season, change in drought frequency based on SPI is projected to decrease over
the upazilas of western part of Bangladesh while the occurrence of drought based on SPI is also
projected to increase over the upazilas of northeastern and southern part under RCP4.5 using
NorESM model rainfall. Mild wet conditions are likely to prevail over the upazilas of central
to western (Rajshahi region) part and mild to moderate dry conditions are expected to occur

`

over the upazilas of northeast (Sylhet regions) and southern part of Bangladesh for 2070 as
presented in Fig. 5.5.

In monsoon season, mild wet conditions are projected to occur in most of the upazilas in
Bangladesh except in some upazilas of western part (Rajshahi regions), it is likely to occur
moderate to severe wet conditions in 2070. On the other hand, mild to moderate dry conditions
are expected over the upazilas of northeastern and southern parts of Bangladesh in the same
year as shown in Fig. 5.5.

5.3.3 Seasonal drought for Post-monsoon season for the year 2070

Post-monsoon season in 2070, Mild wet conditions are excepted to occur in almost all upazilas
of central part and mild dry conditions are likely to prevail over the upazilas of southern and
northeastern parts. Moderate to extreme dry conditions are likely to occur in the upazilas of
northwestern part (Naogaon-Dinajpur regions) of Bangladesh for the year 2070 as presented in
Fig. 5.6.

5.3.4 Seasonal drought for Winter season for the year 2070

In winter season, mild dry conditions are expected to occur in almost all upazilas in Bangladesh
except in a few upazilas of northwestern and southeastern part, where it is likely to prevail
moderate dry conditions under RCP4.5 using NorESM model rainfall for the year 2070 as
presented in Fig. 5.6.

5.4
Projected change in vulnerability index map based on seasonal drought using SPI
for the year 2090
5.4.1 Seasonal drought for Pre-monsoon season for the year 2090

In pre-monsoon season, change in drought frequency based on SPI is projected to decrease over
the upazilas of northwestern part of Bangladesh while the occurrence of drought based on SPI
is also projected to increase over the upazilas of central to eastern part under RCP4.5 using
NorESM model rainfall. Mild wet conditions are likely to prevail over the upazilas
northwestern part and mild to moderate dry conditions are expected to occur over the upazilas
of central, southwestern, southeastern and northeastern part for the year 2090 as presented in
Fig. 5.7.

5.4.2 Seasonal drought for Monsoon season for the year 2090

Drought frequency in monsoon season is likely to decrease in most of the upazilas in
Bangladesh using NorESM model rainfall. Severe to extreme wet conditions are projected to
increase in almost all upazilas (central to southern part) for monsoon season in 2090. Mild to
moderate wet conditions are likely to occur over the upazilas of northern part of Bangladesh as
shown in Fig. 5.7.

`

5.4.3 Seasonal drought for Post-monsoon season for the year 2090

Post-monsoon season in 2090, mild to moderate wet conditions are excepted to occur in most
of the upazilas in northwestern part of Bangladesh and central to southern part upazilas, it is
likely to prevail severe to extreme wet conditions for the year 2090 as presented in Fig. 5.8.

5.4.4 Seasonal drought for Winter season for the year 2090

In winter season, no occurrence of drought based on SPI is projected over Bangladesh under
RCP4.5 using NorESM model rainfall in 2090. Severe to extremely wet conditions are expected
to occur over the upazilas of western part for the year 2090, and mild to moderate wet conditions
are likely to prevail over the upazilas of northeastern and northwestern part for the same year
in Bangladesh as presented in Fig. 5.8.

6.1.11.1 Projected rainfall change for pre-monsoon season for the year 2090

Pre-monsoon season in rainfall is likely to decrease by more than -10% over the upazilas of
southern part of Bangladesh. In the upazilas of north-western part and in some pockets upazilas,
rainfall change is expected to between -10 to 10% compared to the baseline period and some
pockets upazilas rainfall is projected to increase more than 10% under RCP4.5 for the year
2090 as presented in Fig. 6.34.

6.1.11.2 Projected rainfall change for monsoon season for the year 2090

Monsoon season in rainfall is found to be increased by more than 10% in almost all upazilas in
Bangladesh except in some upazilas of northwestern part, where the rainfall change is expected
to between -10 to 10% compared to the baseline period under RCP4.5 for the year 2090 as
presented in Fig. 6.34.

6.1.11.3 Projected rainfall change for post-monsoon season for the year 2090

Post-monsoon season in rainfall is projected to increase by more than 10% in almost all upazilas
in Bangladesh except in a few upazilas of northwestern part, where the rainfall change is
expected to between -10 to 10% compared to the baseline period under RCP4.5 for the year
2090 as presented in Fig. 6.35.

6.1.11.4 Projected rainfall change for winter season for the year 2090

Winter season in rainfall is projected to increase by more than 10% all upazilas in Bangladesh.
compared to the baseline period under RCP4.5 for the year 2090 as presented in Fig. 6.35.

6.1.12 Projected annual rainfall change under RCP4.5 for the year 2090

Annual rainfall is expected to increase by more than 10% in almost all upazilas in Bangladesh
except in some upazilas of northwestern part, where the rainfall change is expected to between
-10 to 10% compared to the baseline period under RCP4.5 for the year 2090 as presented in
Fig. 6.36.

6.2
Projected maximum temperature change vulnerability map under RCP4.5
6.2.1 Projected monthly maximum temperature change under RCP4.5 for the year 2030
6.2.1.1 Projected maximum temperature change for January for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for the month of January is likely to rise by 1.5oC in almost all upazilas
in Bangladesh (Table 6.1) except in some upazilas of southern part, where the maximum
temperature is projected to increase by 0.5-1.0oC for the year 2030 under RCP4.5 as presented
in Fig. 6.37.

6.2.1.2 Projected maximum temperature change for February for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for February is expected to increase by 1.0-2.0oC in almost all upazilas
in Bangladesh (Table 6.1) except in a few upazilas of northeastern part, it is likely to increase
by more than 0.5oC for the year 2030 under RCP4.5 as presented in Fig. 6.37.

6.2.1.3 Projected maximum temperature change for March for the year 2030

The maximum temperature is projected to increase by 1.0-2.0oC all upazilas in Bangladesh
(Table 6.1) for the month of March under RCP4.5 for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.38.

6.2.1.4 Projected maximum temperature change for April for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for the month of April is expected to increase by 1.0-2.0oC in most of
the upazilas of Bangladesh (Table 6.1) with the rise being somewhat moderate (0.0-1.0oC) in a
few upazilas of southeastern part under RCP4.5 for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.38.

6.2.1.5 Projected maximum temperature change for May for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for the month of May is likely to increase by 1.0-2.0oC in almost all
upazilas in Bangladesh (Table 6.1) except in some upazilas of southern part, where the
maximum temperature is found to be in the range of about 0.0-1.0oC in Bangladesh under
RCP4.5 for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.39. The rate of increase of maximum
temperature of northern part upazilas are higher than that of southern part upazilas in
Bangladesh.

6.2.1.6 Projected maximum temperature change for June for the year 2030

The maximum temperature is projected to increase by 1.0-2.0oC all upazilas in Bangladesh
(Table 6.1) for the month of June under RCP4.5 for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.39.

6.2.1.7 Projected maximum temperature change for July for the year 2030

`

Maximum temperature for July is projected to increase about by 1.5oC in almost all upazilas in
Bangladesh (Table 6.1) except in a few upazilas of southern part, its maximum temperature is
likely to rise by 0.5oC under RCP4.5 for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.40.

6.2.1.8 Projected maximum temperature change for August for the year 2030

Maximum temperature during August is likely to increase about by 1.3oC all upazilas in
northern part in Bangladesh (Table 6.1) except in the upazilas of southern part, where the
maximum temperature is projected to rise by about 0.5oC under RCP4.5 for the year 2030 as
presented in Fig. 6.40.

6.2.1.9 Projected maximum temperature change for September for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for September is expected to increase by 1.0-2.0oC in almost all upazilas
in Bangladesh (Table 6.1) except in some pockets upazilas of southwestern part, where the
maximum temperature is projected to rise by more than 0.9oC for the year 2030 as shown in
Fig. 6.41.

6.2.1.10 Projected maximum temperature change for October for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for October is likely to increase by about 1.2oC all upazilas of northern
part of Bangladesh (Table 6.1) except in the upazilas of southern part, where the maximum
temperature is expected to rise by about 0.85oC compared to the baseline period for the year
2030 as shown in Fig. 6.41. The rate of increase of maximum temperature in northern part
upazilas is higher than that of southern part upazilas.

6.2.1.11 Projected maximum temperature change for November for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for November is projected to increase by more than 1.3oC all upazilas
of Bangladesh (Table 6.1) compared to baseline period (1971-2000) under RCP4.5 for the year
2030 as shown in Fig. 6.42.

6.2.1.12 Projected maximum temperature change for December for the year 2030

Maximum temperature for December is projected to increase by more than 1.3oC all upazilas
in Bangladesh (Table 6.1) compared to the baseline period for the year 2030 as shown in Fig.
6.42.

6.3.1.2 Projected minimum temperature change for February for the year 2030

in Bangladesh (Table 6.5) except in some upazilas of southern part, where it is likely to increase
by 0.75oC under RCP4.5 for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.73.

6.3.1.3 Projected minimum temperature change for March for the year 2030

Minimum temperature for the month of March is likely to increase by 1.3oC all upazilas in
Bangladesh (Table 6.5) compared to the baseline period (1971-2000) under RCP4.5 for the
year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.74.

6.3.1.4 Projected minimum temperature change for April for the year 2030

Minimum temperature during April is projected to increase by 1.2oC over the upazilas of
northern part of Bangladesh (Table 6.5). But in the upazilas of southern parts in minimum
temperature is likely to rise by 0.8oC for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.74. The rate of
increase of minimum temperature in the northern part upazilas is the higher than the southern
part upazilas over Bangladesh.

6.3.1.5 Projected minimum temperature change for May for the year 2030

Minimum temperature for the month of May is expected to increase by 1.5oC in the upazilas of
northern part in Bangladesh (Table 6.5). But in the upazilas of southern parts in minimum
temperature is likely to rise by 0.7oC compared to the baseline period under RCP4.5 for the
year 2030 as presented in Fig. 6.75.

6.3.1.6 Projected minimum temperature change for June for the year 2030

Minimum temperature for the month of June is projected to increase by 1.25oC in almost all
upazilas in Bangladesh (Table 6.5) except in some upazilas of southern part, where the
minimum temperature is found to be in the range of about 0.0-1.0oC in Bangladesh for the year
2030 as presented in Fig. 6.75.

CHAPTER 7

PROJECTED CHANGE IN NUMBER OF RAINY DAYS VULNERABILITY MAP
FOR 487 UPAZILAS IN BANGLADESH UNDER RCP4.5 SCENARIO

7.1
Projected change in number of rainy days’ vulnerability map under RCP4.5
7.1.1 Monthly projected change in number of rainy days’ vulnerability map under
RCP4.5 for the year 2030
7.1.1.1 Change in number of rainy days for the month of January for the year 2030

Number of rainy days is an indicator of distribution of rainfall when seen with the amount of
rainfall during a year. No considerable change in the number of rainy days is likely in a majority
of upazilas in Bangladesh. The number of rainy days is expected to decrease in a few upazilas
of Sylhet regions under RCP4.5 for January for the year 2030 as shown in Fig. 7.1.

7.1.1.2 Change in number of rainy days for the month of February for the year 2030

The number of rainy days are expected to be received in fewer days in a majority upazilas in
Bangladesh. The number of rainy days is expected to increase in some isolated upazilas of
southern part, and in some upazilas of western, northwestern, central and southern part is likely
to remain about the same under RCP4.5 for the month of February for the year 2030 as
presented in Fig. 7.1.

7.1.1.3 Change in number of rainy days for the month of March for the year 2030

The number of rainy days are expected to be reduced in all upazilas in Bangladesh compared
to number of long period rainy days (1971-2000) under RCP4.5 for the month of March for
2030 as presented in Fig. 7.2.

7.1.1.4 Change in number of rainy days for the month of April for the year 2030

The rainfall is expected to be received in fewer days in some upazilas of western and southern
part. The number of rainy days is expected to increase in a fewer upazilas in isolated places in
Bangladesh and to remain about the same in the upazilas of northern, eastern as well as some
upazilas of southeastern part under RCP4.5 for the month of April for 2030 as shown in Fig.
7.2.

7.1.1.5 Change in number of rainy days for the month of May for the year 2030

The number of rainy days is expected to increase in a fewer upazilas of northern part and in
some pockets upazilas in southern part in Bangladesh is likely to remain about the same. The
number of rainy days is likely to decrease in the upazilas of southeastern part and in some
pockets upazilas in different places over Bangladesh under RCP4.5 for May for 2030as shown
in Fig. 7.3.

`

7.1.1.6 Change in number of rainy days for the month of June for the year 2030

The rainfall is expected to be received in fewer days in a majority upazilas of western part. The
number of rainy days is expected to increase in northeastern part and some isolated upazilas in
the southeastern part in Bangladesh and to remain about the same in the upazilas of north-south
narrow part and some upazilas in the southeastern part under RCP4.5 during June for 2030 as
presented in Fig. 7.3.

7.1.1.7 Change in number of rainy days for the month of July for the year 2030

The number of rainy days are expected to be received in more days over the upazila of southern
parts and to remain about the same over the upazilas of northern part in Bangladesh and number
of rainy days are expected to be received in fewer days in some upazilas of Chapai Nawabganj
zila (Rajshahi regions) under RCP4.5 for July for the year 2030 as shown in Fig. 7.4.

7.1.1.8 Change in number of rainy days for the month of August for the year 2030

The number of rainy days are projected to remain about the same in almost all upazilas in
Bangladesh except in some upazilas of western part and some isolated upazilas of central part
as well as southeastern part, where the number of rainy days is expected to be received in more
days under RCP4.5 during August for the year 2030 as presented in Fig. 7.4.

7.1.1.9 Change in number of rainy days for the month of September for the year 2030

The number of rainy days are expected to be received in fewer days in almost all upazilas in
Bangladesh except in some pockets upazilas in southeastern part, where the number of rainy
days are projected to remain about the same under RCP4.5 for September for 2030 as shown
in Fig. 7.5.

7.1.1.10 Change in number of rainy days for the month of October for the year 2030

The number of rainy days are expected to be received in more days in almost all upazilas in
Bangladesh except in some upazilas of northwestern and southeastern part, where the number
of rainy days are likely to remain about the same under RCP4.5 for October for 2030 as shown
in Fig. 7.5.

7.1.1.11 Change in number of rainy days for the month of November for the year 2030

The number of rainy days are expected to be received in fewer days in most of the upazilas in
Bangladesh except in some upazilas of northwestern and southeastern part. Northwestern and
in some pockets upazilas of northeastern part, where the number of rainy days are projected to
remain about the same and some isolated upazilas of northwestern and southeastern part, where
the number of rainy days are expected to be received in more days under RCP4.5 during
Number for 2030 as presented in Fig. 7.6.

`

7.1.1.12 Change in number of rainy days for the month of December for the year 2030

CHAPTER 8

MAKING A VULNERABILITY MAP FOR 487 UPAZILAS IN BANGLADESH BY
USING NDVI, TCI, VCI

8.1 Discussion on NDVI

In order to monitor spatial extent of agricultural drought, long term NDVI has been used in this
study. This index is useful for estimation of biomass potential measuring leaf area index (LAI)
and production pattern. Over the years, NDVI has been successfully used by many researchers
in different studies based on vegetation phenology, vegetation classification and mapping of
continental land cover. NDVI is suitable for monitoring drought, estimating healthy status of
vegetation, crop growth conditions and crop yields. The basic concept of NDVI is based on the
fact that internal mesophyll structure of healthy green leaves reflects Near-Infrared (NIR)
radiation whereas the leaf chlorophyll and other pigments absorb a large proportion of the red
visible (VIS) radiation. This function of internal leaf structure becomes reversed in case of
unhealthy or water stressed vegetation.

NDVI = (NIR-VIS)/(NIS+VIS)

NDVI is calculated by the difference between reflectance in near infrared (NIR) and visible red
(VIS) brand of electromagnetic spectrum. The value of NDVI ranges between -1 and +1. NDVI
has been accepted as a popular index for monitoring agricultural drought, estimating soil
moisture and vegetation condition. However, the utility of NDVI for studying vegetation and
related issues may be constrained by several sources of error that usually occur due to
atmospheric noise and many other reason like satellite orbit drift, satellite change and sensor
degradations. Since weather related NDVI fluctuations cannot be detected easily, the ecological
component must be separated from the impact of weather for estimating the actual conditions
of vegetation health. In this context, suggested Vegetation Condition Index (VCI) for
identifying drought related vegetation stress and measuring the intensity, duration, dynamics
and impacts of drought on overall vegetation condition.

NOAA-AVHRR based NDVI has been successfully used for global long term vegetation
trends, categorization and quantification of vegetative drought and estimating the drought
vulnerability. Long term (2007-2018) satellite data from NOAA-AVHRR have been used in
the present study to compute VCI for monitoring the agricultural drought.

8.2
NDVI, VCI, TCI and crops monitoring
Long term NDVI for rain-fed season (July-September) was used in this study. Through NDVI
has been used by many authors for studying drought, it has been recommended by a number of
studies to use VCI rather using NDVI alone. The stacked NDVI layers were visualized to
identify agricultural vigor and differences in vegetation health and TCI during excess, drought
and normal rainfall year as shown in Fig. 8.1 and 8.2. In order to assess the performance of
NOAA-AVHRR derived NDVI for excess, drought and normal rainfall year were chosen for

`

their distinct NDVI characteristics. While visually comparing the NDVI with VCI and TCI for
excess, drought and normal rainfall year. We have selected very recent excess, drought and
normal rainfall year to see the comparing the NDVI, VCI and TCI.

Excess rainfall year: 2017

NDVI is a simple graphical indicator that can be used to analyse remote sensing
measurements and assess whether the target being observed contains live green vegetation or
not. Moderate difference in agricultural vigour was noticed by NDVI at most of the upazilas
over central, western as well as southeastern part and no change in agricultural vigour is also
observed in some isolated upazilas, agricultural vigour was decreased over the upazilas of
northwestern, northeastern and southeastern during excess rainfall year in 2017 as shown in
